## Board Assessment

**Position:**
```
♜ ♞ ⭘ ⭘ ♚ ♝ ⭘ ♜
♟ ⭘ ♕ ⭘ ♞ ♟ ⭘ ♟
⭘ ⭘ ♝ ♟ ⭘ ⭘ ⭘ ⭘

♙ ♙ ⭘ ⭘ ♙ ⭘ ♙ ⭘
♖ ♘ ♗ ⭘ ♔ ♗ ♘ ⭘
```

**Key observations:**
1. The white queen on c7 is deep in my camp and, notably, my king has **zero** legal moves — the queen controls d7/d8.
2. The immediate threat is **Qxd6**, since the d6 pawn is currently undefended.
3. Most importantly: the queen is nearly **trapped**! Let me count her squares:
   - Qxc6+ → Nxc6 (defended by Nb8)
   - Qxb8 → Rxb8 (defended by Ra8)
   - Qb7 → Bxb7 · Qb6 → axb6 · Qa7 → Rxa7
   - Qd7+ → Kxd7 · Qd8+ → Kxd8 (both undefended!)
   - Qc8 → Nxc8 · The c-file below is blocked by my bishop, the h2-diagonal blocked by my d6 pawn.
   
   Her **only** genuinely safe squares are **d6 (capturing) and a5**.

## The Move

So the plan is clear: defend d6, which simultaneously reduces the queen to a single escape square. The knight does this perfectly — **Ne7-f5** covers d6 (and h4), while landing on an active square eyeing e3/g3/d4.

Checks:
- Bc6 remains defended by Nb8, so Qxc6+ still loses the queen.
- If White tries e4 to kick me, **Nd4!** is superb — White has no c-pawn, so c2 is a gaping hole (Nxc2+ picks up the a1 rook).
- If White ignores the queen's plight with a developing move, she remains stuck with only a5.

**Action: `make_move e7f5`**

Let me analyze the position first:

- **White queen on c8** is giving check to my king on **e8** along the 8th rank.
- Can I capture it? Bishop c6 doesn't cover c8, knight b8 can't reach c8, and rook a8 is blocked by my own knight on b8. **No.**
- Can I block? The only square between c8 and e8 is **d8**, and nothing can get there (rook a8 blocked by knight, bishop f8 can't move to d8). **No.**
- So the king must move: 
  - **d8** — controlled by the queen. ✗
  - **d7** — controlled by the queen (c8–d7 diagonal). ✗
  - **f8** — occupied by my own bishop. ✗
  - **e7** — not attacked by the queen (its diagonal runs c8–d7–e6–f5) nor by any bishop, knight, rook or pawn. ✓

So **Ke7 is the only legal move**. As a bonus, it defends my bishop on f8, so if White grabs it with Qxf8+, I simply reply Kxf8 and win the queen.

My reasoning:

   - **Kc3** → **Rxg2**, and the rook is safe with a pawn in the bank.
   - **Kb5** → **Rxg2**; even if White grabs a6, I have R+3P vs 2P.
   - **Kb4** → **Rxg2** again, and White's h3 pawn falls next.

4. **Long-term plan.** After hoovering up g2 and h3, I advance the f/g/h pawn mass supported by my king on g6 and promote. White's lone a-pawn is far too slow, and my rook controls the promotion square from behind.

## Position Assessment

**Black:** King b3, Rook h2, pawns a6, f7, g7, h7
**White:** King c1 (alone)

This is a basic K+R mate. Let me check the tempting candidates:

- **Rh1+?** — Bad! It abandons the 2nd rank, and White escapes with **Kd2** (my king on b3 covers b2/c2 but *not* d2), breaking out of the box.
- **Kc3** — Solid, but White just shuffles b1/d1 and I have to solve a tempo problem to get the opposition with *me* to move.

## Best move: Rd2!

This is the cleanest, most forcing continuation. The rook on d2 holds the entire 2nd rank **and** seals the d-file, so White's king is squeezed into a two-square prison (b1/c1) and **Kb1 is forced**.

Then the mate is a short, forced sequence:

**1...Rd2 Kb1 2...Kc3!** — now White must choose:
- **Kc1** → **Rd1#** (b1 cut by the rook, b2/c2/d2 all covered by my king on c3)
- **Ka1** → **Kb3!** (a2 held by the rook, so b1 is forced) **Kb1 → Rd1#**

Either way it's mate in at most four, with no stalemate risk at any point since b1 always remains available.

**Action: `make_move h2d2`**

**Black:** Re2, Ng3, Kg8, pawns c7, f7, g7, h7

So **Re8 is my only legal move** — and it at least does double duty: it blocks the check *and* attacks the undefended queen on d8 (the Ra1, Rf1, and Bc1 don't cover d8), so White is obliged to find something better than saving the queen.

♜ ♞ ⭘ ⭘ ♚ ♝ ⭘ ♜
♟ ♟ ⭘ ♛ ⭘ ♟ ♟ ♟

I'm already a pawn up with the more active pieces and White's king still stuck in the center — piling on tempo pressure is the right plan.

Both king moves are available, so the check must be escapable either way — but they are not equal in value:

**Why f8 over h8:**

- **f8 steps completely off that diagonal.** From f8, the king is untouched by Qd5 (which controls the d-file, the 5th rank, and the e6/f7 and e4/c4 diagonals) and untouched by Rd1 (the d-file is blocked by my own d7 pawn, so Rd8+ is impossible).
- **f8 also improves my coordination:** the king covers e7/e8/f7, and my rook on a8 stays connected to the back rank once the c8 bishop develops.

**The plan going forward:** I'm up two bishops for nothing. After …b6/…b5 (or …d6 and …Bd7), my c8 bishop breathes, and then my Qc3 + Bb2 battery on the long dark diagonal aims straight at White's exposed king — f2 and g2 are both empty, so threats like …Qe1+ or …Bd4 with mating ideas come fast.
